# Student name aliases

Maps the names that show up in transcript filenames, calendar invites, and email
to the canonical student folder slug in `students/`.

It exists because Gemini names its exports from the meeting title, which is
whatever was typed at booking time: `Sofia`, `sofia`, `Sofia Minassian`,
`LSAT Session between Joshua and Meghan Mullins` all mean one student. Without
this file every pass re-derives the same mapping by hand and gets it slightly
differently.

Read by `system/scripts/transcript-inventory.py`. Format is one bullet per
alias, `alias -> folder-slug`. Matching is case- and punctuation-insensitive,
so only add entries the automatic matcher cannot get on its own (exact slug,
first name, and unambiguous containment are already handled).

# Student roster

**Generated file — do not hand-edit.** Rebuild with:

```
python3 system/scripts/build-roster.py
```

Machine-readable twin: `students/roster.generated.json`, written by the
same run from the same rows. The command center's student picker reads
it through `/api/students`. A folder with no `<slug>.md` page is not a
student and appears in neither file.

Name, email, and status come from the student's own `<slug>.md`
frontmatter (primary, authored). Sessions and last-session come from the
Gemini transcript inventory. To correct a name or email, edit the student's
page and regenerate; editing this file does nothing.

**7Sage** is the second column on purpose: it is a core part of the student's
profile and must never be missed. A linked value means they are set up for the
`7sage-onboarding` / `7sage-update` skills; **⚠️ NOT SET** means their
`7sage_review:` frontmatter URL is missing and they cannot be pulled. The
"Not set up on 7Sage" section below lists every such student.

**Booking columns are read live** from the backend's `booking_passes` on each
rebuild, so they are as fresh as the last run and never a stale local copy.
Revoked passes are not shown: a revoked slug is a dead link.

Last-contact is deliberately absent. Gmail owns it.

# Assign work on LSAT U

Put an Activity, a guided drill, or a message into a student's LSAT U account
from the command line. No browser, no clicking.

## Authorization

Joshua pre-authorized this on 2026-09-07: **a single named student is a go.**
Run it, then report. Do not stop to ask permission first.

Confirm before running when:

- assigning to **3 or more students at once** — each one emails a real person
- the student name is **ambiguous** (the script refuses and lists the matches)
- the student is **Thaniuska Vivas** or **Amanda** — no platform account found
- Joshua names someone absent from [[STUDENT-PLATFORM-IDENTITY-MAP]]

This is a deliberate, narrow exception to his global "draft, never send" rule.
That rule still holds for email, posts, and anything outside LSAT U.

## The command

Run from `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os\projects\lsatu`:

```bash
npx vite-node scripts/assign.ts -- --student "Celia" --activity "Read, Recall, Colour"
```

```bash
npx vite-node scripts/assign.ts -- --student "Lydia" --drill "main-conclusion-1"
```

```bash
npx vite-node scripts/assign.ts -- --student "Dakota" --message "Start with PT105.S2 before Thursday."
```

Flags: `--schedule YYYY-MM-DD` unlocks the work that morning. `--dry-run`
resolves the student and the work and writes nothing — use it when the name is
at all uncertain.

Always `--dry-run` first for a student you have not assigned to before in this
session. It costs one second and it is the only cheap way to catch a wrong
account.

## Why a script and not a direct DB write

`scripts/assign.ts` calls the app's **own** `assignWork()` from
`src/lib/assignWork.ts`. Assigning touches seven tables — the assignment
snapshot, `classroom_items`, the conversation and its participants, `messages`,
`notifications` — plus the email edge function, and it rolls the whole thing
back if the classroom item or notify throws. There is no RPC and no edge
function for it; it is client-side code.

So never hand-write these rows. A second implementation drifts from the UI the
first time the app changes, and produces assignments subtly unlike the ones
Joshua makes by clicking.

## Credentials

Read from `~/ai-os/.env`, which is **not** a git repo:

- `LSATPREP_ADMIN_EMAIL`, `LSATPREP_ADMIN_PASSWORD` — already set

Never put a credential in `projects/lsatu/.env`. That file **is tracked in git**
and would be committed. The script signs in as Joshua and works under normal
admin RLS, the same permission path the UI uses.

`SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` is deliberately **not** used even when present: it
bypasses RLS on every write, so a script running as service_role would hide an
RLS regression instead of hitting it. The script warns and falls back.

## Expected output

```
Assign "Read, Recall, Colour" → Celia <celiacutler@gmail.com>
Assigned. id=e83e2193-…
  inbox ok · bell ok · email ok
```

A notify channel reading `FAILED` is **not** an assignment failure — the work is
in her Classroom and only a message went missing. Report it, do not re-run.

## Failure modes

**`duplicate key … activity_assignments_active_unique`** — she already holds an
active assignment for this Activity. Not a bug; the DB refusing a double-assign.
Check what she has before re-trying (below).

**`"X" is ambiguous`** — the script lists every matching account and stops.
Pick the full name or the email. Never guess: a misdirected assignment is
silent, because she simply never sees it.

**Sign-in failed** — the credentials in `~/ai-os/.env` are wrong or the account
gained MFA.

## Checking what a student already has

```bash
npx vite-node scripts/assign.ts -- --student "Celia" --activity "..." --dry-run
```
resolves her identity. For her actual assignments, query
`activity_assignments` filtered on `student_id`, ordered by `created_at desc`.

## Read, Recall, Colour specifically

Assign the title **"Read, Recall, Colour"** — the section-library door. She then
picks any of 12 seeded sections (PT1.S3, PT21.S3, PT101.S2, PT102.S3, PT103.S1,
PT104.S1, PT105.S1, PT105.S2, PT106.S2, PT112.S4, PT134.S3, PT140.S1), 25 min
each. Every attempt is kept; re-running saves a new attempt.

The script prefers the exact title, so it resolves to the door rather than one
of the 12 sections. Pass a `read-recall-colour-pt*-s*` slug only to pin her to
one section deliberately.

## Related

Vault: `wiki/ASSIGN-AN-ACTIVITY.md` (the browser path and what fires),
`wiki/STUDENT-PLATFORM-IDENTITY-MAP.md` (which account is which student —
33 platform accounts, 20 real students, 13 unmatched).

# Assign an Activity — the runbook

The repeatable path for putting an LSAT U Activity into a student's hands.
Written 2026-09-07 after walking it live on lsatprep.study.

Joshua says **"assign &lt;activity&gt; to &lt;student&gt;"** and this is what happens,
end to end, with no further questions in the normal case.

Companion: [[STUDENT-PLATFORM-IDENTITY-MAP]] — which platform account is which
student. Read it before ticking a name.

## Standing authorization

Joshua granted this on 2026-09-07: assigning an Activity to a **named student**
is a **go** — do it, then report. Do not stop to ask permission first.

This is a deliberate exception to the global "draft, never send" rule, which
still holds for email, posts, and messages. It exists because assignment is the
core loop of the tutoring business and confirming each one made it useless.

**Still confirm first when:**

- assigning to **3 or more students at once** (each one emails a real person)
- the name is **ambiguous** — most importantly **Steven Morris vs "Stevie"**,
  two accounts that may be one person
- the student is on the unresolved list: **Thaniuska Vivas** (no account),
  **Amanda** (no account found)
- Joshua names a student who is **not in the identity map at all**

## The path

The Activity Centre lives at `/activities` — Classroom sidebar → **Study** →
**Activities**. Joshua's account is admin, so each card in **The library** carries
its own assign button.

1. Navigate to `https://lsatprep.study/activities`
2. `find` the button **"Assign &lt;Activity name&gt;"** (one per card, exact title)
3. Click it — a dialog opens, titled **"Assign: &lt;Activity name&gt;"**
4. Optional: fill **"Schedule for"** (a date input) if Joshua named a due date
5. `read_page` the dialog and locate the student's checkbox by display name
6. Click the checkbox — the footer button updates to **"Assign to 1 students"**
7. Click that button
8. Confirm the result, then report what was sent

The dialog is a portal — it renders **outside `<main>`**, so `get_page_text` will
not show it. Use `read_page` (with the dialog's `ref_id` to keep output small).

## The nine Activities

Assign by the exact card title:

| # | World | Activity | Grain |
|---|---|---|---|
| 01 | The Specimen Lab | Vocab Rehab | a word |
| 02 | The Signal Observatory | Spot the Relationship | a sentence |
| 03 | The Switchyard | Conditional Rephrase | a statement, translated |
| 04 | The Polarity Chamber | Negation Station | a statement, negated |
| 05 | The Argument Atelier | Find the Conclusion | an argument |
| 06 | The Debate Chamber | Rebuttal Rephrase | an argument, restated |
| 07 | The Press | Dumb it Down | a paragraph |
| 08 | The Loom | Spot the Callback | a passage |
| 09 | Out Loud | Read, Recall, Colour | a whole section, against the clock |

### Read, Recall, Colour

Assign **card 09** — the section-library door. The student then chooses any of
the 12 seeded sections, each 25 minutes:

PT1.S3 · PT21.S3 · PT101.S2 · PT102.S3 · PT103.S1 · PT104.S1 · PT105.S1 ·
PT105.S2 · PT106.S2 · PT112.S4 · PT134.S3 · PT140.S1

Every attempt is kept. Re-running a section saves a **new attempt**; it never
replaces the old one.

Only assign an individual `read-recall-colour-pt*-s*` slug when Joshua
deliberately wants her pinned to one section. The door is the default.

## What assigning actually does

`assignWork()` — `projects/lsatu/src/lib/assignWork.ts`:

1. **Snapshots** the Activity into `activity_assignments`. The snapshot is
   immutable once issued, so later content edits do not reach an assignment
   already sent.
2. **Writes a `classroom_items` row** — this is what makes it reachable at all.
3. **Notifies on four channels**: inbox message, bell, email, classroom item.

Notification failure never fails the assignment. A student can therefore hold an
assignment she was never told about — worth checking if she says she got nothing.

## Verifying it landed

After assigning, the sheet closes and a toast confirms. To verify properly:
open `/teacher` → the student's record, or have her Classroom show the item
under **Assigned work**.

If she says she cannot see it: check her **Activities access flag** first.
Flags resolve in `projects/lsatu/src/hooks/useUserPermissions.ts`. An assignment
lands regardless of flags, but the surface that displays it may be gated.

## Defects to mention when assigning Read, Recall, Colour

As of 2026-09-07 these are live and unfixed. Tell her, or fix them first:

- The door's heading reads **"Recall Rephrase Colour"** — a name that is not the
  product's — directly under a pill reading "Read, Recall, Colour".
  Hardcoded at `SectionLaunchFrame.tsx:281`; the aria-label at :314 repeats it;
  `SectionLibraryLaunch.test.tsx:54` asserts the wrong string, which is why the
  2,643-test suite stays green.
- Step 1's copy in production is the old *"Intake only…"* wording. The rewrite
  and its migration `20260906220000_simplify_recall_read_step.sql` are
  uncommitted and have never run.
- Attempt dates can render out of order — PT134.S3 shows Attempt 1 dated after
  Attempt 3. This is the `graded_at` vs `created_at` bug named in `PLAN.md`
  item 5.

## Verified working (2026-09-07)

- All 9 Activities seeded and listed
- All 12 RRC sections seeded and runnable
- `attempt_no` **is** applied in production — multi-attempt history renders
- 33 accounts in the picker; 20 map to real students

Related: [[STUDENT-PLATFORM-IDENTITY-MAP]] · [[tasks]] · [[hot]]

1	/**
2	 * Headless assign: put an Activity, a guided drill, or a plain message into a
3	 * student's LSAT U account from the command line.
4	 *
5	 * Run with vite-node so `@/` resolves and this calls the app's OWN assignWork()
6	 * rather than a second implementation of it. That matters: assigning touches
7	 * seven tables (assignment snapshot, classroom item, conversation, message,
8	 * notification, plus the email edge function) and rolls back on failure. A
9	 * reimplementation would drift from the UI the first time the app changed.
10	 *
11	 *   npx vite-node scripts/assign.ts -- --student "Celia" --activity "Read, Recall, Colour"
12	 *   npx vite-node scripts/assign.ts -- --student "Celia" --drill "main-conclusion-1"
13	 *   npx vite-node scripts/assign.ts -- --student "Celia" --message "Start with PT105.S2."
14	 *   npx vite-node scripts/assign.ts -- --student "Celia" --activity "..." --dry-run
15	 *
16	 * Credentials are read from ~/ai-os/.env (git-ignored), never from this repo's
17	 * .env, which IS tracked in git. Same convention as scripts/db-pull.mjs.
18	 */
19	import fs from "node:fs";
20	import path from "node:path";
21	import { fileURLToPath } from "node:url";
22	import { supabase } from "@/integrations/supabase/client";
23	import { assignWork } from "@/lib/assignWork";
24	
25	const REPO = path.resolve(path.dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url)), "..");
26	
27	function readEnv(file: string): Record<string, string> {
28	  const out: Record<string, string> = {};
29	  if (!fs.existsSync(file)) return out;
30	  for (const line of fs.readFileSync(file, "utf8").split(/\r?\n/)) {
31	    const t = line.trim();
32	    if (!t || t.startsWith("#")) continue;
33	    const i = t.indexOf("=");
34	    if (i < 0) continue;
35	    out[t.slice(0, i).trim()] = t.slice(i + 1).trim().replace(/^["']|["']$/g, "");
36	  }
37	  return out;
38	}
39	
40	const env = {
41	  ...readEnv(path.resolve(REPO, "../../.env")),
42	  ...readEnv(path.join(REPO, ".env")),
43	};
44	
45	// ---------------------------------------------------------------- arguments
46	
47	function arg(name: string): string | undefined {
48	  const i = process.argv.indexOf(`--${name}`);
49	  return i >= 0 ? process.argv[i + 1] : undefined;
50	}
51	const has = (name: string) => process.argv.includes(`--${name}`);
52	
53	const studentQuery = arg("student");
54	const activityQuery = arg("activity");
55	const drillQuery = arg("drill");
56	const messageText = arg("message");
57	const scheduleFor = arg("schedule");
58	const dryRun = has("dry-run");
59	
60	if (!studentQuery || (!activityQuery && !drillQuery && !messageText)) {
61	  console.error(
62	    "usage: vite-node scripts/assign.ts -- --student <name|email> \\\n" +
63	      "         (--activity <title> | --drill <slug|title> | --message <text>)\\\n" +
64	      "         [--schedule YYYY-MM-DD] [--dry-run]",
65	  );
66	  process.exit(2);
67	}
68	if (scheduleFor && !/^\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}$/.test(scheduleFor)) {
69	  console.error(`--schedule must be YYYY-MM-DD, got "${scheduleFor}"`);
70	  process.exit(2);
71	}
72	
73	// --------------------------------------------------------------- signing in
74	
75	/**
76	 * Sign in as Joshua. The service_role key would bypass RLS entirely, so it is
77	 * only honoured when explicitly present; the default is a normal admin login,
78	 * which exercises the same permission path the UI does. A script that quietly
79	 * ran as service_role would hide an RLS regression instead of hitting it.
80	 */
81	async function signIn(): Promise<string> {
82	  if (env.SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY) {
83	    console.warn(
84	      "! SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY is set but this script signs in as the admin user.\n" +
85	        "  Using it would require injecting a second client into assignWork, which would\n" +
86	        "  bypass RLS for every write. Falling back to the admin login.",
87	    );
88	  }
89	  const email = env.LSATPREP_ADMIN_EMAIL;
90	  const password = [REDACTED];
91	  if (!email || !password) {
92	    console.error(
93	      "assign: LSATPREP_ADMIN_EMAIL / LSATPREP_ADMIN_PASSWORD are not set.\n" +
94	        "  Add them to ~/ai-os/.env (git-ignored). This repo's .env is TRACKED in git,\n" +
95	        "  so a credential placed there would be committed.",
96	    );
97	    process.exit(2);
98	  }
99	  const { data, error } = await supabase.auth.signInWithPassword({ email, password });
100	  if (error || !data.user) {
101	    console.error(`assign: sign-in failed — ${error?.message ?? "no user returned"}`);
102	    process.exit(1);
103	  }
104	  return data.user.id;
105	}
106	
107	// -------------------------------------------------------------- resolution
108	
109	interface StudentRow {
110	  class_id: string;
111	  display_name: string | null;
112	  email: string | null;
113	  [k: string]: unknown;
114	}
115	
116	/**
117	 * Students come from the admin-manage-users edge function, which cross-joins
118	 * profiles with auth.users for the email. `class_id` is the canonical student
119	 * identifier everywhere in this codebase.
120	 */
121	async function listStudents(): Promise<StudentRow[]> {
122	  const { data, error } = await supabase.functions.invoke("admin-manage-users", {
123	    method: "GET",
124	  });
125	  if (error) throw new Error(`admin-manage-users failed: ${error.message}`);
126	  const rows = (data ?? []) as StudentRow[];
127	  if (!Array.isArray(rows)) throw new Error("admin-manage-users returned no user list");
128	  return rows;
129	}
130	
131	/**
132	 * Resolve a name or email to exactly one student. Ambiguity is fatal rather
133	 * than resolved by picking the first: sending a student's homework to the wrong
134	 * account is silent — she simply never sees it, and he never learns why.
135	 */
136	function resolveStudent(rows: StudentRow[], query: string): StudentRow {
137	  const q = query.trim().toLowerCase();
138	  const exact = rows.filter(
139	    (r) => r.email?.toLowerCase() === q || r.display_name?.toLowerCase() === q,
140	  );
141	  const pool = exact.length ? exact : rows.filter(
142	    (r) =>
143	      r.display_name?.toLowerCase().includes(q) || r.email?.toLowerCase().includes(q),
144	  );
145	
146	  if (pool.length === 0) {
147	    console.error(`assign: no student matches "${query}".`);
148	    console.error("Known:", rows.map((r) => r.display_name ?? r.email).join(" · "));
149	    process.exit(1);
150	  }
151	  if (pool.length > 1) {
152	    console.error(`assign: "${query}" is ambiguous — ${pool.length} accounts match:`);
153	    for (const r of pool) console.error(`  - ${r.display_name ?? "(no name)"}  <${r.email ?? "?"}>`);
154	    console.error("Narrow it with the full name or the email address.");
155	    process.exit(1);
156	  }
157	  return pool[0];
158	}
159	
160	async function resolveActivity(query: string) {
161	  const q = query.trim();
162	  const bySlug = await supabase.from("activities").select("id,title,slug,mode").eq("slug", q).maybeSingle();
163	  if (bySlug.data) return bySlug.data;
164	
165	  const { data, error } = await supabase
166	    .from("activities")
167	    .select("id,title,slug,mode")
168	    .ilike("title", `%${q}%`);
169	  if (error) throw error;
170	  const rows = data ?? [];
171	
172	  // "Read, Recall, Colour" matches the door and all 12 sections. Prefer the
173	  // exact title, which is the door — the surface that lets her pick a section.
174	  const exact = rows.filter((r) => r.title.toLowerCase() === q.toLowerCase());
175	  const pool = exact.length ? exact : rows;
176	
177	  if (pool.length === 0) {
178	    console.error(`assign: no Activity matches "${query}".`);
179	    process.exit(1);
180	  }
181	  if (pool.length > 1) {
182	    console.error(`assign: "${query}" matches ${pool.length} Activities:`);
183	    for (const r of pool) console.error(`  - ${r.title}  (${r.slug})`);
184	    process.exit(1);
185	  }
186	  return pool[0];
187	}
188	
189	async function resolveDrill(query: string) {
190	  const q = query.trim();
191	  const bySlug = await supabase.from("guided_drills").select("id,title,slug").eq("slug", q).maybeSingle();
192	  if (bySlug.data) return bySlug.data;
193	
194	  const { data, error } = await supabase
195	    .from("guided_drills")
196	    .select("id,title,slug")
197	    .ilike("title", `%${q}%`);
198	  if (error) throw error;
199	  const rows = data ?? [];
200	  if (rows.length === 0) {
201	    console.error(`assign: no guided drill matches "${query}".`);
202	    process.exit(1);
203	  }
204	  if (rows.length > 1) {
205	    console.error(`assign: "${query}" matches ${rows.length} drills:`);
206	    for (const r of rows) console.error(`  - ${r.title}  (${r.slug})`);
207	    process.exit(1);
208	  }
209	  return rows[0];
210	}
211	
212	// ---------------------------------------------------------------- messaging
213	
214	/** Mirrors findOrCreateThread in src/lib/classroomNotify.ts, which is private. */
215	async function findOrCreateThread(adminUserId: string, studentId: string): Promise<string> {
216	  const { data: mine } = await supabase
217	    .from("conversation_participants")
218	    .select("conversation_id")
219	    .eq("user_id", adminUserId);
220	  const { data: theirs } = await supabase
221	    .from("conversation_participants")
222	    .select("conversation_id")
223	    .eq("user_id", studentId);
224	
225	  const ids = new Set((mine ?? []).map((r) => r.conversation_id));
226	  const shared = (theirs ?? []).find((r) => ids.has(r.conversation_id));
227	  if (shared) return shared.conversation_id as string;
228	
229	  const { data: conv, error } = await supabase
230	    .from("conversations")
231	    .insert({})
232	    .select("id")
233	    .single();
234	  if (error || !conv) throw error ?? new Error("could not create conversation");
235	
236	  const { error: pErr } = await supabase.from("conversation_participants").insert([
237	    { conversation_id: conv.id, user_id: adminUserId },
238	    { conversation_id: conv.id, user_id: studentId },
239	  ]);
240	  if (pErr) throw pErr;
241	  return conv.id as string;
242	}
243	
244	// --------------------------------------------------------------------- main
245	
246	async function main() {
247	  const teacherId = await signIn();
248	  const student = resolveStudent(await listStudents(), studentQuery!);
249	  const who = `${student.display_name ?? "(no name)"} <${student.email ?? "?"}>`;
250	
251	  if (messageText) {
252	    console.log(`Message → ${who}\n  "${messageText}"`);
253	    if (dryRun) return console.log("\n(dry run — nothing sent)");
254	    const conversationId = await findOrCreateThread(teacherId, student.class_id);
255	    const { error } = await supabase
256	      .from("messages")
257	      .insert({ conversation_id: conversationId, sender_id: teacherId, body: messageText });
258	    if (error) throw error;
259	    return console.log("Sent.");
260	  }
261	
262	  const item = activityQuery
263	    ? { kind: "activity" as const, activityId: (await resolveActivity(activityQuery)).id }
264	    : { kind: "guided_drill" as const, drillId: (await resolveDrill(drillQuery!)).id };
265	
266	  const label = activityQuery
267	    ? (await resolveActivity(activityQuery)).title
268	    : (await resolveDrill(drillQuery!)).title;
269	
270	  console.log(`Assign "${label}" → ${who}${scheduleFor ? `  (unlocks ${scheduleFor})` : ""}`);
271	  if (dryRun) return console.log("\n(dry run — nothing written)");
272	
273	  const { ok, failed } = await assignWork({
274	    teacherId,
275	    studentIds: [student.class_id],
276	    item,
277	    scheduledForLocalDate: scheduleFor,
278	  });
279	
280	  if (failed.length) {
281	    console.error(`FAILED: ${failed[0].error}`);
282	    process.exit(1);
283	  }
284	  const r = ok[0];
285	  const n = r.notify;
286	  console.log(
287	    `Assigned. id=${r.assignmentId}\n` +
288	      `  inbox ${n?.inbox_ok ? "ok" : "FAILED"} · bell ${n?.bell_ok ? "ok" : "FAILED"} · email ${n?.email_ok ? "ok" : "FAILED"}`,
289	  );
290	  if (n && !(n.inbox_ok && n.bell_ok && n.email_ok)) {
291	    console.warn("! The work IS assigned and visible; only a notification channel failed.");
292	  }
293	}
294	
295	main()
296	  .then(() => process.exit(0))
297	  .catch((e) => {
298	    console.error(e instanceof Error ? e.message : e);
299	    process.exit(1);
300	  });
301

import * as React from 'react';
import { Check, ChevronDown, Minus, Plus } from 'lucide-react';
import { Link } from 'react-router-dom';
import { Popover, PopoverContent, PopoverTrigger } from '@/components/ui/popover';
import { cn } from '@/lib/utils';

/**
 * How long a section runs, expressed as a percentage of the student's own
 * standard clock rather than as a number of minutes.
 *
 * Percent is the unit a student trains against — "94%" is an intensity, "33
 * minutes" is trivia — and it stays meaningful when the standard clock is not
 * 35:00, which is exactly the case for anyone with extra time. Minutes are
 * derived at the point of use.
 */
export type SectionTiming =
  | { mode: 'timed'; percent: number }
  | { mode: 'unlimited' };

export const STANDARD_TIMING: SectionTiming = { mode: 'timed', percent: 100 };

/**
 * Presets under 100%. Deliberately not evenly spaced: 97 and 94 are the small
 * squeezes a student can absorb without changing method, and 70 is the one
 * that forces a different way of working.
 */
const SPEED_TRAINING_PERCENTS = [97, 94, 70];

const MIN_PERCENT = 10;
const MAX_PERCENT = 300;
/** One nudge of the clock stepper. Half a minute reads cleanly at every base. */
const TIME_STEP_SECONDS = 30;

/** MM:SS, zero-padded on both halves, which is how a section clock reads. */
export function formatClock(totalSeconds: number): string {
  const safe = Math.max(0, Math.round(totalSeconds));
  const minutes = Math.floor(safe / 60);
  const seconds = safe % 60;
  return `${String(minutes).padStart(2, '0')}:${String(seconds).padStart(2, '0')}`;
}

const clampPercent = (p: number) => Math.min(MAX_PERCENT, Math.max(MIN_PERCENT, p));

interface Props {
  value: SectionTiming;
  /**
   * Accepts an updater as well as a value, so the ± steppers can compose off
   * the latest state. With a plain value two fast clicks both read the same
   * render's percent and the second one is lost.
   */
  onChange: React.Dispatch<React.SetStateAction<SectionTiming>>;
  /**
   * The student's own standard clock, in minutes. 35 for a section run at
   * national time; their accommodated clock otherwise. Every percentage on
   * this control is a percentage of THIS, not of 35:00, so a student with
   * time and a half still sees 100% as their normal.
   */
  baseMinutes: number;
  /**
   * Questions in the chosen section. Omit before a section is picked — the
   * per-question line then hides rather than quoting an invented count.
   */
  questionCount?: number;
  /**
   * Restricts the control to these percentages, in the order given, and hides
   * speed training and custom. Reading Comprehension needs this: its clock is
   * a per-passage budget, so it can only run the paces that budget divides
   * into, and offering an arbitrary percentage would be a lie.
   */
  allowedPercents?: number[];
  /** Overrides the row label for a percentage (RC names its three paces). */
  labels?: Record<number, string>;
  /** Hide the Unlimited row where a stopwatch has no meaning. */
  allowUnlimited?: boolean;
  /**
   * Line under the options naming the student's standing extra time, with a
   * way to change it. Extra time is who a student is, not a decision they
   * should re-make before every section — this states it and links out.
   */
  accommodationNote?: React.ReactNode;
  className?: string;
  /**
   * Classes for the dropdown surface. The section launcher paints a dark card
   * inside a light theme, so its Selects carry their own surface treatment and
   * this control has to take the same one or it opens as a white slab on top.
   */
  contentClassName?: string;
}

/**
 * The section clock, as a single dropdown.
 *
 * Reads top to bottom the way the decision is actually made: the default

 * arbitrary number last. Every row states its consequence in the two units
 * that matter — the clock the student will see, and the seconds per question
 * that clock buys them.
 */

}: Props) {

import { supabase } from '@/integrations/supabase/client';
import { BOOTCAMPS } from '@/lib/bootcamps';
import { createClassroomItem } from '@/lib/classroomItems';
import type { ClassroomItemType } from '@/hooks/useClassroomItems';
import { notifyStudentOfClassroomItem, type NotifyResult } from '@/lib/classroomNotify';
import { parseLocalDay } from '@/lib/localDate';
import { coerceStudentLevel, type StudentLevel } from '@/lib/studentLevel';

/**
 * The one thing a teacher can hand a student. Every assign surface in the
 * workspace narrows to this union, so there is exactly one place that knows
 * how work reaches a student.
 */
export type AssignableItem =
  | { kind: 'homework_set'; setId: string }
  | { kind: 'guided_drill'; drillId: string }
  | { kind: 'activity'; activityId: string }
  | { kind: 'bootcamp'; topicId: string };

export interface StudentResult {
  studentId: string;
  /**
   * The assignment row. Absent for bootcamps, which have no row of their own.
   * On a bundle this is the first item's row; see `assignmentIds` for all of them.
   */
  assignmentId?: string;
  classroomItemId?: string;
  route?: string;
  /** Every assignment row written for this student, in bundle order. */
  assignmentIds?: string[];
  /** Every classroom item created for this student, in bundle order. */
  classroomItemIds?: string[];
  /**
   * Per-channel notify outcome. A false channel means the work landed and the
   * student can see it, but one message did not go out.
   */
  notify?: NotifyResult;
  /** Set only on `failed`: why this student got nothing. */
  error?: string;
}

export interface AssignWorkResult {
  ok: StudentResult[];
  failed: StudentResult[];
}

/** What one kind of item needs, resolved once before the per-student loop. */
interface AssignPlan {
  type: ClassroomItemType;
  title: string;
  description?: string;
  /** Wording for the notification ("New guided drill", etc). */
  assignmentType?: string;
  sourceTable: string;
  /** Insert the assignment row. Returns null for kinds that have no row. */
  insertRow: (studentId: string) => Promise<string | null>;
  sourceId: (assignmentId: string | null) => string | undefined;
  route: (assignmentId: string | null) => string;
  unlockAt: string | null;
  dueAt: string | null;
  metadata: Record<string, unknown>;
}

function message(error: unknown, fallback: string) {
  return error instanceof Error ? error.message : fallback;
}

/** Per-student proficiency, resolved once for the whole assign. */
export type StudentLevelMap = ReadonlyMap<string, StudentLevel | null>;

/**
 * Postgres 42703 (undefined_column), which PostgREST also surfaces in the
 * message. Means the migration has not been applied to this database yet.
 */
function isMissingColumnError(error: unknown): boolean {
  const e = error as { code?: string; message?: string } | null;
  if (!e) return false;
  if (e.code === '42703') return true;
  return /proficiency_level/.test(e.message ?? '') && /does not exist|column/i.test(e.message ?? '');
}

/**
 * One practice test, in progress.
 *
 * This page is the invigilator, not the exam. It says which section is next,
 * starts it, runs the break, reads each leg's result back out of the tables the
 * runners write, and scores the sitting at the end. Every actual question is
 * answered somewhere else, in the same runner the student uses outside a test.
 *
 * The result readback is deliberately conservative: a leg with no matching row
 * is reported as unfinished rather than scored as zero. A zero here would read
 * on the score report as "answered everything wrong", which is a different and
 * much more discouraging claim than "you walked away from section 2".
 */
import * as React from "react";
import { useNavigate, useParams } from "react-router-dom";
import { Loader2 } from "lucide-react";
import { Button } from "@/components/ui/button";
import { useAuth } from "@/contexts/AuthContext";
import {
  formatPracticeTestClock,
  legLabel,
  loadPracticeTestRun,
  practiceTestPace,
  practiceTestRaw,
  practiceTestScaled,
  savePracticeTestRun,
  PRACTICE_TEST_BREAK_SECONDS,
  type PracticeTestRun as Run,
} from "@/lib/practiceTest";
import { lrLegLaunch, rcLegHref, readLegResult } from "@/lib/practiceTestRunner";
import "./testing-centre.css";

function mmss(seconds: number) {
  const s = Math.max(0, Math.floor(seconds));
  return `${Math.floor(s / 60)}:${String(s % 60).padStart(2, "0")}`;
}

/**
 * What a ticking clock says out loud.
 *
 * The digits change twice a second, and a live region wired straight to them
 * would talk over itself for ten minutes without pause. Coarse steps instead:
 * whole minutes while minutes remain, then ten-second steps inside the last
 * one. The string changes only when the step does, so the region speaks only
 * when there is something new to say.
 */
function breakAnnouncement(remaining: number): string {
  const seconds = Math.ceil(remaining);
  if (seconds <= 0) return "The break is over. Continue when you are ready.";
  if (seconds <= 60) return `${Math.ceil(seconds / 10) * 10} seconds of the break left.`;
  const minutes = Math.ceil(seconds / 60);
  return `${minutes} ${minutes === 1 ? "minute" : "minutes"} of the break left.`;
}

/** The break, on its own clock. Skippable — it is a break, not a gate. */
function BreakScreen({ run, onDone }: { run: Run; onDone: () => void }) {
  const startedAt = run.breakStartedAt ? Date.parse(run.breakStartedAt) : Date.now();
  const [remaining, setRemaining] = React.useState(() =>
    Math.max(0, PRACTICE_TEST_BREAK_SECONDS - (Date.now() - startedAt) / 1000),
  );

import { supabase } from '@/integrations/supabase/client';
import type { Json } from '@/integrations/supabase/types';
import type { ClassroomItem } from '@/hooks/useClassroomItems';

interface CreateClassroomItemInput {
  studentId: string;
  createdBy: string;
  type: ClassroomItem['type'];
  title: string;
  description?: string;
  contentMarkdown?: string;
  contentHtml?: string;
  sourceTable?: string;
  sourceId?: string;
  unlockAt?: string | null;
  dueAt?: string | null;
  priority?: number;
  status?: ClassroomItem['status'];
  visibleToStudent?: boolean;
  metadata?: Record<string, unknown>;
}

export async function createClassroomItem(input: CreateClassroomItemInput) {
  const { data, error } = await supabase
    .from('classroom_items')
    .insert({
      student_id: input.studentId,
      created_by: input.createdBy,
      type: input.type,
      title: input.title,
      description: input.description ?? null,
      content_markdown: input.contentMarkdown ?? null,
      content_html: input.contentHtml ?? null,
      source_table: input.sourceTable ?? null,
      source_id: input.sourceId ?? null,
      unlock_at: input.unlockAt ?? null,
      due_at: input.dueAt ?? null,
      priority: input.priority ?? 0,
      status: input.status ?? 'assigned',
      visible_to_student: input.visibleToStudent ?? true,
      metadata_json: (input.metadata ?? {}) as Json,
    })
    .select()
    .single();
  if (error) throw error;
  return data as ClassroomItem;
}

/** Read one item back, e.g. to show what an assign just created. */
export async function getClassroomItem(id: string) {
  const { data, error } = await supabase.from('classroom_items').select().eq('id', id).maybeSingle();
  if (error) throw error;
  return data ? (data as ClassroomItem) : null;
}

export async function updateClassroomItem(
  id: string,
  patch: Partial<{
    title: string;
    description: string | null;
    status: ClassroomItem['status'];
    unlock_at: string | null;
    due_at: string | null;
    visible_to_student: boolean;
    completed_at: string | null;
    priority: number;
    metadata_json: Record<string, unknown>;
  }>,
) {
  const { metadata_json, ...rest } = patch;
  const dbPatch =
    metadata_json !== undefined
      ? { ...rest, metadata_json: metadata_json as Json }
      : rest;
  const { data, error } = await supabase
    .from('classroom_items')
    .update(dbPatch)
    .eq('id', id)
    .select()
    .single();

Tool result:
// Local calendar-date helpers.
//
// Day boundaries for streaks and daily stats must follow the student's local
// timezone, not UTC. `new Date().toISOString().slice(0,10)` returns the UTC
// calendar date, so any evening practice in a US timezone lands under
// "tomorrow" and breaks streak/day-boundary math. Use these instead.

// Format a Date as its LOCAL YYYY-MM-DD (en-CA yields ISO-style order).
export function localDayISO(date: Date = new Date()): string {
  const y = date.getFullYear();
  const m = String(date.getMonth() + 1).padStart(2, '0');
  const d = String(date.getDate()).padStart(2, '0');
  return `${y}-${m}-${d}`;
}

// Parse a YYYY-MM-DD calendar date into that day's LOCAL midnight.
//
// `new Date('2026-08-05')` is parsed as UTC midnight, which in any western
// timezone is the evening of the 4th. Scheduling work for "the 5th" that way
// unlocks it a day early. Splitting the parts and using the Date(y, m, d)
// constructor keeps the day the day the teacher picked.
export function parseLocalDay(iso: string): Date | null {
  const match = /^(\d{4})-(\d{2})-(\d{2})$/.exec(iso.trim());
  if (!match) return null;
  const [, y, m, d] = match;
  const date = new Date(Number(y), Number(m) - 1, Number(d));
  return Number.isNaN(date.getTime()) ? null : date;
}

// Local date N days offset from `from`, formatted YYYY-MM-DD. Uses setDate so
// it stays correct across DST transitions (unlike subtracting 86_400_000 ms).
export function addLocalDays(days: number, from: Date = new Date()): string {
  const d = new Date(from);
  d.setDate(d.getDate() + days);
  return localDayISO(d);
}

## user

Base directory for this skill: C:\Users\Joshu\.claude\skills\consultation-followup-drafts

You are Joshua's email drafting partner for aspiringattorneys.com (LSAT tutoring). Each run, find consultations that just happened and draft a personalized follow-up email from the meeting transcript, for Joshua to review. NEVER send. Only create Gmail drafts.

STEP 1 — Read voice + templates (required):
- Read C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/archive/wiki-pre-rebuild-2026-07-16/domains/lsat-u/knowledge/email-voice-principles.md
- Read C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/archive/wiki-pre-rebuild-2026-07-16/domains/lsat-u/knowledge/post-consultation-follow-up-template.md
- Read C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/email-hq/Consultation Follow-Up Automation.md
Follow the voice and the template exactly. Prose only, no bullets beyond the short options/steps list, no em dashes, under ~150 words.

STEP 2 — Find consultations ready for follow-up. Use the Google Calendar connector (list_events) to list events whose title contains "Consultation" from the last 4 days that are already in the past (ended before now). For each, look for a "Notes by Gemini" attachment or a linked Transcript Google Doc on the event. If there is no such attachment yet, SKIP it (the call may not have happened or notes aren't ready).

STEP 3 — Read the transcript. For each event that has Gemini notes, use the Google Drive connector (read_file_content) with the attached doc's file ID. Extract: the student's name and email (from the event attendees), the details to mirror (diagnostic/current score, target score, study platform, exam timeline, main struggles), and the "Next steps" action items.

STEP 4 — Check it isn't already handled. Use the Gmail connector (search_threads) to find any existing thread with that attendee. If a next-steps / follow-up email has already been sent to them since the consultation date, SKIP (one follow-up per consultation). If they already booked and paid, draft the short onboarding note from the template instead of the sales-y next-steps email.

STEP 5 — Draft the follow-up using the template. Default to Variant A (affirming); use Variant C (tiered options menu) if the notes show the student was cost-sensitive or comparing options. Personalize the recap line with a real detail from the transcript. Include the correct links (session booking, 5-hour package, platform signup, registration form) per the template. Greeting "Hi [First name],". Sign "Best,\nJoshua". Create it as a Gmail draft with create_draft, replying in the existing thread (replyToMessageId) if one exists, otherwise a new draft to the attendee's email. Do NOT send.

STEP 6 — Report: for each consultation, the student's name/email, the detail you mirrored, which variant you used, and that a draft is waiting in Gmail for review. If no consultations were ready, say so in one line. Flag anything uncertain (e.g. couldn't tell if they already booked) so Joshua can check before sending.


ARGUMENTS: to Amanda M. Huynh an find the email who sent em an emai lrecently who is whitney she sent me one earlier today show me the draft for bot hof them

gws — Google Workspace CLI

USAGE:
    gws <service> <resource> [sub-resource] <method> [flags]
    gws schema <service.resource.method> [--resolve-refs]

EXAMPLES:
    gws drive files list --params '{"pageSize": 10}'
    gws drive files get --params '{"fileId": "abc123"}'
    gws sheets spreadsheets get --params '{"spreadsheetId": "..."}'
    gws gmail users messages list --params '{"userId": "me"}'
    gws schema drive.files.list

FLAGS:
    --params <JSON>       URL/Query parameters as JSON
    --json <JSON>         Request body as JSON (POST/PATCH/PUT)
    --upload <PATH>       Local file to upload as media content (multipart)
    --upload-content-type <MIME>  MIME type of the uploaded file (auto-detected from extension if omitted)
    --output <PATH>       Output file path for binary responses
    --format <FMT>        Output format: json (default), table, yaml, csv
    --api-version <VER>   Override the API version (e.g., v2, v3)
    --page-all            Auto-paginate, one JSON line per page (NDJSON)
    --page-limit <N>      Max pages to fetch with --page-all (default: 10)
    --page-delay <MS>     Delay between pages in ms (default: 100)

SERVICES:
    drive                Manage files, folders, and shared drives
    sheets               Read and write spreadsheets
    gmail                Send, read, and manage email
    calendar             Manage calendars and events
    admin-reports        Audit logs and usage reports (also: reports)
    docs                 Read and write Google Docs
    slides               Read and write presentations
    tasks                Manage task lists and tasks
    people               Manage contacts and profiles
    chat                 Manage Chat spaces and messages
    classroom            Manage classes, rosters, and coursework
    forms                Read and write Google Forms
    keep                 Manage Google Keep notes
    meet                 Manage Google Meet conferences
    events               Subscribe to Google Workspace events
    modelarmor           Filter user-generated content for safety
    workflow             Cross-service productivity workflows (also: wf)
    script               Manage Google Apps Script projects

ENVIRONMENT:
    GOOGLE_WORKSPACE_CLI_TOKEN               Pre-obtained OAuth2 access token (highest priority)
    GOOGLE_WORKSPACE_CLI_CREDENTIALS_FILE    Path to OAuth credentials JSON file
    GOOGLE_WORKSPACE_CLI_CLIENT_ID           OAuth client ID (for gws auth login)
    GOOGLE_WORKSPACE_CLI_CLIENT_SECRET       OAuth client secret (for gws auth login)
    GOOGLE_WORKSPACE_CLI_CONFIG_DIR          Override config directory (default: ~/.config/gws)
    GOOGLE_WORKSPACE_CLI_KEYRING_BACKEND     Keyring backend: keyring (default) or file
    GOOGLE_WORKSPACE_CLI_SANITIZE_TEMPLATE   Default Model Armor template
    GOOGLE_WORKSPACE_CLI_SANITIZE_MODE       Sanitization mode: warn (default) or block
    GOOGLE_WORKSPACE_PROJECT_ID              Override the GCP project ID for quota and billing
    GOOGLE_WORKSPACE_CLI_LOG                 Log level for stderr (e.g., gws=debug)
    GOOGLE_WORKSPACE_CLI_LOG_FILE            Directory for JSON log files (daily rotation)

1	---
2	title: Teaching Voice

7	status: active
8	subject: Teaching Voice
9	aliases: []
10	---
11	
12	# Teaching Voice
13	
14	How LSAT U sounds, across every surface. This is the canonical voice doc: route all student-facing and brand copy through it. Built from a grill with Joshua (2026-06-13), then tested and enriched against the full corpus of his real correspondence (944 messages he authored, mid-2025 to mid-2026, from the Gmail takeout; 2026-07-18). Claims below marked with a quote are evidenced by that corpus.
15	
16	It replaces the old two-register / 15-named-tools "preserve verbatim" profile (deleted 2026-06-10) and folds in the brand/marketing register the earlier draft was missing.
17	
18	## Core voice (every register)
19	
20	The constant under all three registers below. This is Joshua on the page.
21	
22	- **Quiet authority.** State the value and let it land. Never oversell, never beg, never perform enthusiasm.
23	- **Specific, never generic.** Real scores, the actual skill, the student's own history. Praise is a specific observation, not "great job."
24	- **Honest about the work, gentle about the person.** Be blunt that the LSAT is hard ("expect this to be exhausting and very challenging"). Never be blunt about a student's shortcomings. Honesty points at the material; warmth points at the person. When someone slips, nudge gently, never accuse.
25	- **Light, grounded metaphor, understated in email.** In his real correspondence the recurring image is quiet: "partner with you on your LSAT journey," "getting set up," "foundational." The vivid images (runway, climb, march toward November, an easy door to walk through) belong to authored teaching and brand copy, not to email. Keep metaphor light and grounded; never float off into abstraction.
26	- **Balanced rhythm.** Short declaratives plus one slightly longer warm beat. Air between ideas. Each idea gets its own line. Real messages are short and single-purpose (median ~280 characters).
27	- **Plain professional diction.** No corporate jargon, no startup hype ("unlock," "crush," "level up," "game-changer" — "crush" appears once in 944 messages). Contractions are the norm, not the exception. Exclamation points are genuine, not performed: a warm message often carries one, occasionally more, especially in openers ("Sorry for such a long delay!"). He is not exclamation-averse.
28	- **Forward-looking.** End on the next step or what's ahead.
29	- **No em dashes, ever.** Use periods, colons before lists, the middot (·) as a separator, and numbered steps for procedures.
30	
31	## Openings, closings, and signature phrases
32	
33	The mechanical fingerprint of his email, from the real corpus. Match these when drafting as Joshua.
34	
35	- **Greeting.** "Hi [Name]," is the default (used ~519x). "Hey [Name]," for established/returning students (~229x, warmer). "Hello [Name]," (~105x) and "Dear [Name]," (~18x, formal) for first contact or a more formal note.
36	- **Sign-off.** "Best," and "Best regards," are the workhorses (~360x / ~237x). "Thanks," when it's a favor or logistics (~146x). "Warmly," / "Warm regards," (~54x) for a genuinely warm note. "Talk soon," (~9x) for someone he'll see again soon.
37	- **Signature.** Almost always signed **"Joshua"** (844 of 944 messages). The name is near-universal; include it.
38	- **Signature phrases (his verbal fingerprint, use naturally, don't overuse):** "let me know" (by far the most common, ~390x), "looking forward," "I hope" / "hope all is well" / "hope you," "no worries at all," "happy to" / "more than happy to," "just wanted to make sure," "make sure," "no rush," "of course."
39	- **Punctuation he actually uses:** semicolons (correctly, ~41x); ellipses essentially never. (For em-dashes, the hard rule in Core voice governs — never.)
40	
41	## The apology / accountability move
42	
43	One of his most frequent and characteristic behaviors, and worth naming on its own. When he is late to reply, or missed or mishandled something, he opens by owning it plainly, often with a brief honest reason, then pivots straight to the fix. No over-apologizing, no excuses dressed up.
44	
45	> I'm so sorry. I thought I had let you know ... I clearly didn't communicate that as well as I should have.
46	
47	> I've been away from work for personal reasons, but I'm back now and getting caught up.
48	
49	The pattern: own it in the first line, give the real reason in one clause, move to what happens next. Warm, candid, brief.
50	
51	## Situational micro-templates
52	
53	Verbatim-grounded reference lines for the situations that recur most. Adapt, never paste blindly.
54	
55	- **Student reschedules / cancels:** low-pressure, no guilt.
56	  > No worries at all. It happens, so please don't worry about it. If you would like to reschedule, just let me know.
57	- **He missed / no-showed (approved make-good template):** full ownership, real reason, offer to make it right (see the apology move above). When the cause was an unexpected health issue, the approved shape is: sincerely apologize, state that a serious health scare unexpectedly took him out of commission, offer a **completely free, full LSAT session**, and hand them the complimentary booking link `https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/complimentary-session`. Emphasis stays limited: bold the complimentary offer, italicize the line that one focused session can put points on the board. Draft in Gmail; never send without Joshua's explicit go-ahead.
58	- **Consultation reminder (day-of):**
59	  > Just checking in before our consultation today. Looking forward to speaking with you. I just wanted to make sure everything is still good.
60	- **Consultation follow-up:** warm, specific to their situation.
61	  > Really nice meeting you. I enjoyed our conversation ... I'm glad we got to talk through where you're at and what you're aiming for on test day.
62	- **Inbound prospect (hasn't booked):** state availability plainly, no overselling, hand them the next step.
63	  > Thanks for reaching out ... I'd be happy to talk more about what you're working on and see if we'd be a good fit. You can schedule a consultation here.
64	- **Student under real-life stress:** meet the person first.
65	  > Please don't apologize ... it sounds like you are dealing with an incredibly stressful and demanding situation.
66	
67	## The three registers
68	
69	Which voice governs which surface.
70	
71	### 1. Coaching (earnest) -- inbox, recaps, in-drill feedback, email
72	
73	Fully earnest. Warm, sincere, calm, patient. No jokes. The closest thing to being in the room with Joshua. Normalizes struggle, then moves to the work.
74	
75	Reference line (target tone):
76	
77	> Totally normal to trip on this one. Let's slow down: what is the author actually concluding? Once you see it, the trap answers lose their grip. You've got this.
78	
79	- **Encouragement (student missed a hard one):** honest first, then forward. Name that it's genuinely hard, stay calm, move to the work. Not soft-reassuring, not cold.
80	  > Missing this is expected. It's one of the hardest moves on the test. Let's slow down and see where it slipped.
81	- **Accountability (student isn't doing the work):** gentle nudge, low pressure. Frame it as a slip, find a rhythm together. Never "the reps aren't happening," never tough love.
82	- **Praise (student nails it):** name the exact skill, tie it to their history, raise the bar.
83	  > That's the cleanest you've split premise from conclusion yet. Let's push it into the harder sets.
84	- **Hard truth about the test:** state it plainly. "Do not expect for it to come easy. Expect to find this exhausting."
85	
86	### 2. Teaching content (earnest substance, wit at the edges) -- bootcamp lessons, drill explanations
87	
88	The substance stays earnest and clear (you are explaining a hard skill). The wit lives only at the edges: headers, openers, transitions. No jokes mid-explanation, nothing that costs comprehension.
89	
90	> Header: "The flaw hides in plain sight."
91	> Body: earnest, clear, step by step.
92	
93	### 3. Brand (sharp wit) -- landing, auth, marketing
94	
95	Confident and clever, the way a good lawyer is clever. Wit tied to the real skill, not wordplay for its own sake. Playful is allowed, but every clever line must be earned and true. Hollow cleverness fails (the killed "the next card is the page in front of you" was clever but empty).
96	
97	> Most prep teaches the test. We teach the tell.
98	
99	Brand copy speaks as the platform ("we", "you"), not as an individual ("I", "Joshua"). The product is the coach; no single person narrates the marketing.
100	
101	## Don't
102	
103	- No cold-testing or gotcha tone. (Ties to teach-first-calibrate-after.)
104	- No condescension or empty cheerleading. Encouragement must sit on real substance.
105	- No generic praise, no hollow cleverness, no hedging ("you might want to").
106	- No hype/startup vocabulary.
107	- No em dashes, ever.
108	
109	Corrections log: [[COPY-CORRECTIONS]] records every student-facing wording fix and
110	the reason it was wrong. Read it before writing or editing LSAT U copy.
111	
112	Related: teach-first-calibrate-after, LSAT U. Source samples: `raw/templates/Golden Email Template Playbook.md`, `raw/templates/email_writing_examples_11_full.txt`, and the full authored-email corpus from the Gmail takeout (944 messages, analyzed 2026-07-18; takeout at `raw/sources/private/takeout/`, git-ignored).
113

7:
8:# Aspiring Attorneys — the business
9:
10:Authored context — what the business is and how it runs. One authoritative home (moved from `wiki/entities/aspiring-attorneys.md`, 2026-07-17); the router points here. Source of the underlying answers: `aios/aios-intake.md` (Q1–Q7, 2026-07-16).
11:
12:North star: **automate everything around the sessions so Joshua only shows up and teaches.**
13:
14:## What is sold, to whom
15:
16:- Personalized 1-on-1 LSAT tutoring/coaching: individual sessions, and five-hour / ten-hour packages **with between-session coaching included**. All paid upfront — no package-balance model exists.
17:- Standard post-consultation offers are a single session at **$45/hour**, a five-hour package at **$40/hour**, and a ten-hour package at **$35/hour**.
18:- In the prospect-facing standard follow-up, show only those hourly rates. Do not display the five-hour or ten-hour package totals. The workflow still validates the underlying total charged by each payment link before scheduling and sending.
19:- Label the single-session link `Schedule here`. Label the five-hour and ten-hour package links `Purchase here`.
20:- Post-consultation emails present all three standard offers with a verified action link for each only when no special arrangement exists. A verified person-specific arrangement replaces the standard menu entirely; never mix bespoke terms with unrelated standard offers.
21:- A special arrangement exists only when Joshua explicitly states it, including an explicit statement preserved in the consultation notes or email thread. Never infer a discount or bespoke offer from financial concerns, fee-waiver status, geography, historical links, or other circumstances.
22:- The standard consultation follow-up time is **3:00 PM Toronto time on the next calendar day, including weekends and statutory holidays**, after confirming the consultation occurred, usable notes exist, no follow-up was already sent, and the prospect has not paid.
23:- Consultation follow-ups are scheduled automatically and send at the standard time unless Joshua stops or changes them beforehand. This approval-by-silence rule applies only to the bounded consultation follow-up workflow.
24:- Only one or two lines in a consultation follow-up are personalized from the notes. They prioritize the prospect's goal, test timeline, and central LSAT problem; scores appear only when central, and sensitive details are not repeated merely to demonstrate personalization. The lines must sound specific and human, not generic or AI-written.
25:- Do not include a breathing-room line claiming Joshua waited a couple of days or gave the prospect time to think. The standard follow-up sends the next calendar day, and the email must describe timing truthfully.
26:- If the prospect uses 7Sage and their 7Sage profile URL is not already available, the consultation follow-up asks them to send the URL.
27:- The request must specifically ask for the URL copied from the 7Sage Analytics page. Accept only the canonical `https://7sage.com/u/usr_<id>/review` form as an analytics URL. Drill-builder links, settings pages, and login-token URLs are not substitutes; login-token URLs must be treated as credentials and rejected.
28:- Place the conditional 7Sage Analytics request beside the LSAT U signup step near the end of the consultation follow-up. It is a locked conditional line, not one of the one or two AI-personalized recap lines.
29:- Variant C in `email system/Post-Consultation Follow-Up Template.md` is the canonical consultation follow-up base when no special arrangement exists. Workflow automation may fill its bounded personalization, 7Sage, pricing, and verified-link fields, but should not invent a replacement sales structure.
30:- Remove the application-form step from Variant C. Consultation follow-ups must not send Google Forms or `gformsapp.com` links; intake belongs inside LSAT U during onboarding.
31:- The consultation follow-up includes LSAT U account creation near the end of its next steps using `https://lsatprep.study`. A prospect may sign up before payment because Joshua controls student permissions. Intake, permissions, and teaching access still begin only after confirmed payment.
32:- Send the consultation follow-up inside the existing consultation conversation when a usable Gmail thread exists. Otherwise, start a new message with the fixed subject `Next steps + tutoring options`.
33:- Confirmed payment, not an email reply, triggers onboarding. Standard payments resolve through Stripe for packages and Stripe-linked Cal.com for single sessions; a verified special arrangement uses its stated authority. Cancel any scheduled consultation follow-up if payment arrives before its send time.
34:- Automatic consultation follow-ups fail closed unless the recipient, current prospect state, prices, and price-to-link mapping all verify. Reject placeholders, malformed URLs, form editor links, login-token URLs, self-addressed tests, and one payment link reused across distinct paid tiers without an explicit arrangement. Run validation before scheduling and again before sending.
35:- Verified standard links: single session, `https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/session-link` at $45 for 70 minutes; five-hour package, `https://buy.stripe.com/4gMaEY05F3dc9x13NqdZ61x` at $200 total. No verified $350 ten-hour payment link is currently recorded. Historical ten-hour links that charge $500 must not be used for the current standard offer.
36:- Students in the **United States and Canada**.
37:- Also building **LSAT U** (lsatprep.study) — the online prep platform; code and product docs live in `projects/lsatu/`.
38:
39:## Money and booking
40:
41:- **Stripe** — packages; **Cal.com (Stripe-linked)** — individual paid sessions and free consultations; **e-transfer** rare (Canada).
42:- All sessions land on **Google Calendar** and are conducted on **Google Meet**.
43:- Tracking today is mainly the Stripe dashboard. **Gap:** no single per-student view of payments and sessions-used vs purchased — Cal.com answers sessions-used; a mirror view belongs in `students/` as `*.generated.md`, never a Markdown ledger.
44:- **Self-scheduling links are situational.** Send the appropriate booking link, instead of proposing times, when a student has paid for a session but has not scheduled it, needs to reschedule, or has paid for a package with sessions to book. Do not apply this as a blanket rule to every prospect or email.
45:- **Billing is contextual.** Before drafting a billing or payment reply, reconstruct the specific person's payment source, purchase type, amount or package, relevant agreement, session history, and timeline from the student file and authoritative live systems. Different students can legitimately have different arrangements. Do not force a generic billing answer onto incomplete facts.
46:
47:## Channels
48:
49:- **Gmail (contact@aspiringattorneys.com)** is the main channel — consultations, student threads, follow-ups. The **LSAT U in-app inbox** (Supabase) is a growing second. WhatsApp/iMessage negligible. Solo operator — no team.
50:- Google Meet generates **full transcripts + notes after every session**, arriving by Gmail, attached to the calendar event, and in Drive — the reliable ingest signal for `students/<name>/`.
51:
52:## The weekly time-sucks (ranked)
53:
54:1. **Post-session work** — notes, homework, assigning work, study schedules.
55:2. **Session prep** — per-student, analytics-informed (7Sage + platforms). Target state: prep is "nearly mindless"; the system reviews everything and prepares the session.
56:3. **Pre/post-consultation email workflow** — partly covered by the `consultation-*` skills.
57:4. Conducting sessions — the one part Joshua keeps.
58:

1	# Post-Consultation Follow-Up Template
2	
3	Use this after a consultation call, to move someone from "we talked" to "booked and onboarded." Follow voice-principles.md. Warm, affirming, momentum toward a clear next step. Built from ~25 real follow-ups over the past 8 months.
4	
5	**Assets / links**
6	- First session booking: https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/session-link
7	- Consultation (if they still need one): https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/consultation
8	- 5-hour package (Stripe): https://buy.stripe.com/6oUaEY6u3bJI5gLcjWdZ61N
9	- Platform signup: https://lsatprep.study
10	- Registration / Application form: https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSeYOqa8N1gVX1k5Qqebnv_PJgypVcIT6EbHhMeAyduhrGTYaQ/viewform
11	- Pricing tiers: Single Session $45/hr · 5-Hour Package $40/hr · 10-Hour Package $35/hr
12	- Sign-off used on these: "Best,\nJoshua" (or "Warmly,\nJoshua\nAspiring Attorneys | LSAT Instructor" on the fuller version)
13	
14	---
15	
16	## The arc every follow-up follows
17	
18	1. **Greeting** — "Hi {{first_name}},"
19	2. **Warm recap** — it was great meeting you + affirm them ("I could tell right away you're ambitious and taking this seriously" OR "I hope the consultation was helpful and you took something useful away").
20	3. **"Here are the next steps:"** — the booking/payment options.
21	4. **LSAT U signup** — ask them to create an account at https://lsatprep.study.
22	5. **7Sage Analytics URL when applicable** — if they use 7Sage and the URL is unknown, ask them to send the URL copied from their Analytics page.
23	6. **Payment-triggered close** — confirmed payment triggers onboarding; the prospect does not need to report the payment by email.
24	7. **Sign-off.**
25	
26	---
27	
28	## Variant A — Affirming (default, warm first-contact after consult)
29	
30	> Hi {{first_name}},
31	>
32	> Really nice meeting you. I enjoyed our conversation, and I could tell right away you're ambitious and taking this seriously. I'm glad we got to talk through where you're at right now and what you're aiming for on test day.
33	>
34	> Like I promised, here are the next steps:
35	>
36	> Option 1: Schedule a single session here:
37	> https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/session-link
38	>
39	> Option 2: If you'd like to move forward with the 5-hour package, use this link:
40	> https://buy.stripe.com/6oUaEY6u3bJI5gLcjWdZ61N
41	>
42	> The 5-hour package also includes access to the platform, drills, and LSAT materials we'll be using together.
43	>
44	> If you move forward, please also sign up here:
45	> https://lsatprep.study
46	>
47	> Once you've chosen the option that works best for you and signed up, reply here and let me know. I'll send over the full onboarding instructions so we can start strong.
48	>
49	> Best,
50	> Joshua
51	
52	## Variant B — Value-and-momentum (softer, when you want less hard-sell)
53	
54	> Hi {{first_name}},
55	>
56	> It was a pleasure meeting you. I hope the consultation was helpful and that you were able to take something useful away from our conversation. I'm excited about what we can do with clear direction, consistent work, and dedication.
57	>
58	> Like I promised, here are the next steps:
59	>
60	> 1. Schedule your first session here:
61	> https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/session-link
62	>
63	> 2. Complete this short registration form so I can get everything set up on my end:
64	> https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSeYOqa8N1gVX1k5Qqebnv_PJgypVcIT6EbHhMeAyduhrGTYaQ/viewform
65	>
66	> Once you've scheduled and submitted the form, just reply to this email and let me know. I'll send over the onboarding details so everything is smooth and efficient from day one.
67	>
68	> Best,
69	> Joshua
70	
71	## Variant C — Tiered options menu (when they're price-sensitive or comparing)
72	
73	> Hi {{first_name}},
74	>
75	> I hope you're doing well.
76	>
77	> It was great meeting you. I really enjoyed our conversation, and I'm confident we can build a strong, systematic approach that gets you feeling sharp and prepared for your exam.
78	>
79	> Here are the options to get started:
80	>
81	> - Single Session ($45/hr): Schedule here
82	> - 5-Hour Package ($40/hr): Purchase here
83	> - 10-Hour Package ($35/hr): Purchase here
84	>
85	> After you book, please fill out this short form so I can tailor a focused plan to your goals:
86	>
87	> - Application Form: Complete here
88	>
89	> Once you've booked and submitted the form, just reply to this email and I'll send over your next steps.
90	>
91	> Warmly,
92	> Joshua
93	> Aspiring Attorneys | LSAT Instructor
94	
95	---
96	
97	## Follow-on: onboarding after they book + pay
98	
99	Once they confirm payment and submit the form, you send a short confirmation that kicks off work:
100	
101	> Hi {{first_name}},
102	>
103	> That's great news. I've confirmed your {{package}} and I appreciate you completing the form. We'll get started right away. Here's your first {{step: diagnostic / homework / materials}} below.
104	>
105	> {{link or attachment}}
106	>
107	> {{If a diagnostic:}} Please complete the section linked below. I've applied 150% time for this task, so when you start, {{instruction}}.
108	>
109	> Best,
110	> Joshua
111	
112	---
113	
114	## Rules for the drafter (human or automated)
115	
116	- Pick the variant to match the person: A for most warm conversions, B when you want a lighter touch, C when cost/comparison is on their mind.
117	- Do not claim that Joshua waited multiple days or gave the prospect time to think. The standard follow-up sends at 3:00 PM Toronto time on the next calendar day.
118	- Mirror one real detail from the consultation if you have it (goal, timeline, score, what they struggle with).
119	- Reply inside the existing consultation thread; don't start a new one.
120	- Prose plus the short labeled option/step list only. No em dashes. Keep under ~150 words.
121	- Double-check the correct links are pasted (session vs consultation vs Stripe) before sending.
122	- Label the single-session action `Schedule here`. Label each package-payment action `Purchase here`.
123	- If they've gone quiet, a gentle check-in works ("just checking in to say hello and let you know I'm still really looking forward to our sessions").
124

      --sanitize <TEMPLATE>  Sanitize API responses through a Model Armor template. Requires cloud-platform scope. Format: projects/PROJECT/locations/LOCATION/templates/TEMPLATE. Also reads GWS_SANITIZE_TEMPLATE env var.
      --body <TEXT>          Reply body (plain text, or HTML with --html)

  -a, --attach <PATH>        Attach a file (can be specified multiple times)
      --cc <EMAILS>          CC email address(es), comma-separated
      --bcc <EMAILS>         BCC email address(es), comma-separated
      --html                 Treat --body as HTML content (default is plain text)
      --dry-run              Show the request that would be sent without executing it
      --draft                Save as draft instead of sending
  -h, --help                 Print help

EXAMPLES:
  gws gmail +reply --message-id 18f1a2b3c4d --body 'Thanks, got it!'
  gws gmail +reply --message-id 18f1a2b3c4d --body 'Looping in Carol' --cc carol@example.com
  gws gmail +reply --message-id 18f1a2b3c4d --body 'Adding Dave' --to dave@example.com
  gws gmail +reply --message-id 18f1a2b3c4d --body '<b>Bold reply</b>' --html
  gws gmail +reply --message-id 18f1a2b3c4d --body 'Updated version' -a updated.docx
  gws gmail +reply --message-id 18f1a2b3c4d --body 'Draft reply' --draft

TIPS:
  Automatically sets In-Reply-To, References, and threadId headers.
  Quotes the original message in the reply body.
  --to adds extra recipients to the To field.
  Use -a/--attach to add file attachments. Can be specified multiple times.
  With --html, the quoted block uses Gmail's gmail_quote CSS classes and preserves HTML formatting. Use fragment tags (<p>, <b>, <a>, etc.) — no <html>/<body> wrapper needed.
  With --html, inline images in the quoted message are preserved via cid: references.
  Use --draft to save the reply as a draft instead of sending it immediately.
  For reply-all, use +reply-all instead.

## user

Tool result:
Consultation  between Joshua and Cameron Stribling
Invited  
Attachments 
Meeting records  


Summary
Tutoring session established structural reading methodologies and analytic foundations for upcoming law school entrance exam preparation.Current Prep ChallengesPrevious test preparation strategies failed to yield desired score improvements due to reliance on intuitive guessing rather than systematic analysis. Target goals require transitioning from superficial reading to deep comprehension.Reading Comprehension StrategyThe Read, Recall, and Rephrase method facilitates true passage internalization by simplifying complex referential terms. Predicting argumentative flows and replacing ambiguous pronouns maintains clarity throughout the examination.Argumentation and MethodologySuccessful test performance requires distinguishing factual claims from opinions to isolate conclusions effectively. Establishing a repeatable, systematic framework for logical reasoning ensures consistency compared to ineffective vibe based test taking.


Decisions
Aligned
Analytics review mandated for engagement planning The tutor will analyze the student's current performance analytics prior to establishing a specific session schedule or engagement plan.
Centralized engagement platform adoption The engagement workflow is strictly centralized to utilize the tutor's proprietary platform for all scheduling, communication, and resource management.


Next steps
[Cameron] Share 7sage URL: Send the profile or analytics link for the 7sage platform to the tutor. This allows the tutor to review performance data and identify learning gaps.
[Joshua Fisseha] Send follow-up: Provide all necessary details including platform information, session notes, and assignment structure. Include relevant links for scheduling and direct communication.


Details
LSAT Preparation History: Cameron discussed their previous experience with the Blueprint "170 plus guaranteed" course, which they felt did not produce the desired results, noting that they received a 145 on their initial diagnostic assessment and the same score on their actual August LSAT. Cameron is now utilizing 7Sage and aims to improve their score to the 163 range by fully dedicating their time between August and November. Joshua Fisseha remarked that while high-volume test preparation programs can be useful, they often employ extreme requirements that may filter for individuals who were already predisposed to score highly regardless of the program (00:02:28).
Practice Question Analysis: Joshua Fisseha invited Cameron to work through a practice question to observe their natural analytical process (00:03:54). Cameron tentatively identified "B" as the correct answer choice but initially struggled to summarize the passage in their own words, prompting Joshua Fisseha to guide them through a more detailed analysis (00:04:41).

Reading Comprehension Methodology: Joshua Fisseha introduced the "RNR" method—read, recall, and rephrase—to help Cameron move from simply reading words to achieving true comprehension (00:08:35). They encouraged Cameron to internalize passages by explaining them as if speaking to a friend, rather than adopting a formal persona, and emphasized the need to predict the flow of arguments, such as anticipating a shift after reading words like "normally" (00:09:25).
Referential Phrasing: Joshua Fisseha explained that the LSAT often uses deceptive referential terms like "this," "they," and "that" to compact complex ideas, which can cause students to lose track of the argument. They instructed Cameron to replace these referential words with the specific concepts they represent to maintain clarity throughout the passage (00:12:05).
Identifying Arguments: Joshua Fisseha outlined a two-step process for identifying the conclusion of an argument: the statement must be an opinion, and it must receive support from at least one other statement (00:14:01). They clarified that factual statements, which are verifiable descriptions of the world, cannot serve as an argument's conclusion, as conclusions must be interpretations of facts (00:14:56).
Analyzing the Practice Stimulus: In reviewing the specific practice question, they determined that the second sentence was a factual claim regarding household thresholds, whereas the third sentence, containing the word "evidently," was an opinion (00:16:45) (00:19:49). Through further analysis of the subsequent lines, they established that the final sentence served to support the third sentence, confirming that the third sentence was the passage's conclusion (00:21:39).
Systematic vs. Intuitive Approach: Joshua Fisseha warned against relying on "vibe-based" test-taking, noting that while intuitive guessing can sometimes yield correct results, it lacks consistency and leads to errors when faced with complex questions (00:24:26). They emphasized the importance of a repeatable, systematic approach to ensure test-taking results are reliable rather than dependent on instinct (00:25:31).

Admissions Goals: Cameron expressed a goal of achieving a score in the mid-to-high 160s, identifying Howard University as a primary target and the University of Michigan as a stretch goal (00:27:27). Cameron noted they have a 3.4 GPA and significant professional work experience, which they hope will support their applications (00:29:46).
Areas of Difficulty: Cameron identified specific challenges with causal reasoning and consistently confusing necessary conditions with sufficient conditions. They noted that they often struggle to correctly process the relationship between cause and effect when drilling questions (00:31:39) (00:33:54).
Feasibility of Goals: Joshua Fisseha discussed the possibility of reaching Cameron's target score, stating that while they cannot offer guarantees, significant improvement is attainable if the student remains coachable and maintains a strong work ethic (00:36:14). Joshua Fisseha shared their own experience of struggling with the test initially and emphasized that understanding the underlying structure of the LSAT is crucial for success (00:39:39).
Tutoring Logistics: Regarding the number of sessions required, Joshua Fisseha explained that they could not provide an exact figure without reviewing Cameron's analytics, though sessions typically occur one to two times per week and may decrease as the student becomes more proficient (00:41:01) (00:42:33). Joshua Fisseha committed to sending a follow-up email with details about their platform, which facilitates direct messaging, session recaps, and access to guided drills (00:43:15).
